Chapter 1
Introduction
Gauge theories play a fundamental role in all of modern fundamental physics, but are
at the same time enigmatic and challenging. The aim of this lecture is to provide an
introduction to their quantum version, in the form of quantum gauge ﬁeld theories. It
turns out that a suitable language for these kind of theories is the path integral, which
will be used throughout.
The paradigmatic classical gauge theory is electrodynamics. However, the range of
gauge theories is much vaster than this. As the corresponding classical gauge theories
are the starting point of a path-integral quantization, these will be investigated in some
detail in chapter 2. It is also important to understand the classical solutions, which will
be discussed in chapter 3.
Only in chapter 4 will Yang-Mills theory be quantized, as this requires much more
techniques due to the non-Abelian nature. In fact, it will only be possible at this stage
to quantize the theory at the level of perturbation theory. However, as both in practice
and for the sake of concept formation perturbation theory is invaluable, this is anyhow a
necessary step. This will be continued in chapter 5, where various perturbative processes
will be discussed in detail. As at loop level also in gauge theories divergences arise, this will
not be possible without an extensive discussion of renormalization and infrared problems.
After this, it is possible to delve into gauge theories beyond perturbation theory in
chapter 6. This issue is indeed challenging, and not entirely solved until today. This
will lead also to questions what are the physical degrees of freedom of gauge theories
beyond perturbation theory, and will show that gauge symmetry leads to deeper ontological
problems as well. Here QED will be again a role model, as it is possible to spell out both
the problem and the solution in this case explicitly, although at the price of exponentially
more complicated calculations. It will then be seen how this problem translates to non-
Abelian gauge theories, and where the resolution becomes even more involved.
1

2
This lecture will not be able to cover actual calculations beyond perturbation theory,
as the necessary methods are quiet involved, and thus deserve lectures of their own. These
are available for lattice gauge theory and for functional methods, although this is not an
exhaustive list of possible methods. Some other methods will be encountered in more
specialized lectures.
Finally, in contrast to non-gauge theories, which can always be quantized even if they
are trivial, this is not the case for gauge theories. This is due to anomalies, which will
therefore be treated in chapter 7, and will round out the discussion of gauge theories. This
will show that the integral measure in the path integral can play a more important role
than it usually appears to have.
However, gauge theories are a vast subject, and thus many issues can only be touched
upon. Especially, concrete realizations of gauge theories as theories of, e. g., the strong
force and weak force require their own dedicated lecture. There is also a vast literature on
gauge theories. In the preparation of this course, the following books and review articles
have been used:
• Peskin et al. “An introduction to quantum ﬁeld theory”, Perseus
• B¨ohm et al. “Gauge theories”, Teubner
• O’Raifertaigh, “Group structure of gauge theories”, Cambridge
• Weinberg, “The quantum theory of ﬁelds I+II”, Cambridge
• DeWitt, “The global approach to quantum ﬁeld theory”, Oxford
• Haag, “Local quantum physics”, Springer
• Lavelle et al., “Constituent quarks from QCD”, Phys. Rept. 279
• Maas, “Gauges bosons at zero and ﬁnite temperatures”, Phys. Rept. 524
• Maas, “Brout-Englert-Higgs physics”, PPNP 106
Of course, these are my personal choices, and not necessarily the best ones to learn the
topic from. Thus, I strongly recommend that any student should test various books and
choose the one best suited for their own purposes.

Chapter 2
Gauge theories
As will be seen, gauge theories come in diﬀerent types. However, in all cases, in the end
the same geometrical structures appear, just their geometric complexity diﬀers. Thus, it is
always helpful to start out with the theory with the simplest geometrical structure. As will
be seen, this is the quantum version of Maxwell theory, i. e. a theory just of electric ﬁelds
and magnetic ﬁelds. In fact, this theory is trivial in the sense that it is not interacting.
Thus, akin to the case of the free scalar and fermions, this will be the simplest example
of a gauge theory.
It is worth mentioning, and is is shown in the lecture quantum ﬁeld theory I, that every
theory describing elementary particles with a spin greater than 1/2 seems to necessarily
involve gauge degrees of freedom, if a local description, i. e. one in which no multi-position
integrals appear in the action, is required. Why this is so is as of yet unknown. But what
this implies will be discussed in great detail in due course.
2.1
Global symmetries
Before discussing gauge symmetries it is useful to repeat a few statements about global
symmetries. This will be taken up again in more details in section 2.4, but some prelimi-
nary remarks are necessary for the following.
In the simplest case at the classical level, a symmetry is any (inﬁnitesimal) transfor-
mation of a set of ﬁelds φa(x)
φa →φ
′a = φa + δAabφb = (1 + δA)abφb
(2.1)
such that the action is invariant. It is important here that it is suﬃcient that the action
is invariant, not the Lagrangian itself. Thus, the Lagrangian can be allowed to change by
a total derivative, which vanishes upon integration. Herein δ is an inﬁnitesimal, constant
3

4
2.1.
Global symmetries
parameter, and the matrix A is usually required to be Lie-algebra-valued for a ﬁxed choice
of Lie algebra, but this depends on the action in question. Consider, as the arguably
simplest case, the Lagrangian for N interacting, real scalar ﬁelds
L = 1
2φa(∂2 + m2)φa −λ(φaφa)2.
(2.2)
This so-called linear σ-model is invariant for transformations in which 1 + δA is an O(N)
matrix. Such a symmetry is called global, as it changes the ﬁelds at every space-time
point in the same way. Since it does not aﬀect space-time it acts in an internal space and
is thus called an internal symmetry. In contradistinction, the gauge symmetry of classical
electrodynamics is called a local symmetry, as it changes the vector potential at every
space-time point in a diﬀerent way.
From such a situation follows Noether’s theorem. This theorem is one of the central
reasons why symmetries are so important in physics, as it states that there is a conserved
charge associated with any such global symmetry. Such conserved quantities are of cen-
tral importance for both theoretical and practical reasons. Practical, because exploiting
conservation laws is very helpful in solving problems.
Theoretical, because conserved
quantities deﬁne the properties of a system. In addition, the generalized Wigner-Eckart
theorem allows to deduce complicated tensor structures based on such symmetries alone.
Essentially, Noether’s theorem boils down to the fact that if the action is invariant
under the transformation, and thus a variation of a generalized coordinate, the variation
can be used to derive a conserved quantity. If A is an element of a Lie algebra a ﬁnite
transformation is obtained by exponentiation, yielding
φ
′a =
 eδAab φb =
 eδAφ
a
Because A is hermitian, it deﬁnes an observable in a quantum theory. It is thus usually
called the associated charge operator, and the observables are the charge. Indeed, electric
charge will be deﬁned in this way later.
But for the moment the aim is to see that this implies a conserved quantity.
To
construct it, it is necessary to analyze the transformation properties of the Lagrangian
under the inﬁnitesimal transformation, which is given by
δL = ∂µKµ,
i. e. zero up to a total derivative is allowed, as mentioned above. Of course, Kµ = 0 is also
allowed. It must also hold that
δL = ∂L
∂φaδφa +
∂L
∂∂µφa∂µ(δφa) = ∂µKµ.

Any quantity not invariant under the symmetry will be made up from tensor products
of representations of the Lie algebra or Lie group, and thus has to obey the Wigner-
Eckart theorem. This essentially says that any quantity transforming non-trivially can be
rewritten in terms of the invariant tensors of the symmetry. E. g.
φaφb =

δabf

where f is invariant under transformations. It is an exercise in group theory to ﬁnd the full
structure for more complex objects. The quantity f, which is invariant under symmetry
transformations, is usually called a form factor or dressing function.
If the symmetry is not exact for whatever reason, this becomes more involved. This
is beyond the scope of this lecture, and more can be learned in the lecture on (beyond)
standard model physics. Here, except for the case of anomalies, all symmetries will be
assumed to be unbroken.
2.2
Classical Maxwell theory
Switching now from theories with a global symmetry to gauge symmetries, the archetypal
gauge theory is Maxwell theory.
This is the theory of electromagnetic ﬁelds without

matter. It is encapsulated in the classical Lagrangian
L
=
−1
4FµνF µν
(2.4)
Fµν
=
∂µAν −∂νAµ
with the gauge ﬁeld Aµ and the ﬁeld-strength tensor Fµν. From it the three-dimensional
electric ﬁeld and magnetic ﬁeld can be derived as
Ei
= F0i = −Fi0 =
∂0Ai −∂iA0
(2.5)
Bi
= ϵijkFjk =
ϵijk∂jAk
(2.6)
with the Latin indices running only over the spatial components.
This theory is a gauge theory, because its Lagrangian is invariant under the so-called
gauge transformation
Aµ →Ag
µ = Aµ + ∂µg(x)
(2.7)
with g(x) an arbitrary function. This is similar to (2.1), except for the appearance of
a function rather than a constant. Because the transformation is thus diﬀerent at every
space-time point, this is called a local, or gauge, symmetry.
Because the g(x), being
ordinary functions, are commuting at every space-time point, this is called an Abelian
gauge theory.
It is noteworthy that Fµν, and thus ⃗E and ⃗B, are invariant under gauge transformations,
i. e. they do not change when a gauge transformation is applied,
F g
µν = ∂µAg
ν −∂νAg
µ = Fµν + ∂µ∂νg(x) −∂ν∂µg(x) = Fµν.
A quantity which behaves like this is called gauge-invariant . Since gauge transforma-
tions are a free choice, and thus gauge-dependent quantities depend on this choice, gauge
invariance is a necessary condition for a quantity to be physical. Especially, since the
electromagnetic ﬁelds are measurable, they need to be gauge-invariant.
The classical equations of motion for this theory are the Maxwell equations
∂µF µν
=

(2.8)

=
0,
(2.9)
where the latter identity is sometimes also called (a) Jacobi identity. The quantity ∗Fµν =
ϵµνρσF ρσ is called also the Hodge dual or the dual ﬁeld strength tensor.
Both follow
immediately from the antisymmetry of the ﬁeld strength tensor.

Chapter 2.
Gauge theories
7
The gauge freedom (2.7) can be used to implement conditions, so-called gauge condi-
tions, on the ﬁeld. A general gauge condition can be formulated as a functional C[Aµ, x]
fulﬁlling
C[Aµ, x] = 0
E. g., the Landau (or in the classical case also Lorentz and sometimes radiation) gauge is
deﬁned as
CLandau[Aµ, x] = ∂µAµ = 0.
(2.10)
I. e. the gauge freedom (2.7) is used to transform any given gauge ﬁeld such that the
condition (2.10) applies.
It is not possible to implement any gauge condition. It is only possible, if for any
arbitrary ﬁeld conﬁguration Aµ a function g(x) exists such that C[Ag
µ, xµ] = 0 can be
satisﬁed. Depending on the form of C[Aµ, xµ] guaranteeing this can be an exercise in
algebra, (partial) diﬀerential equations, or worse.
To see that the Landau gauge condition is well deﬁned, consider
0 = ∂µAg
µ = ∂µAµ + ∂2g.
This is a wave equation for g with an inhomogeneity. Since the theory of partial diﬀerential
equations implies that there exists a solution for any inhomogeneity, this implies that the
Landau gauge is well deﬁned.
Note, however, that it may happen that a gauge condition does not fully ﬁxes the gauge
freedom. E. g., the Landau gauge condition does not do so, as any gauge transformation
satisfying a free-wave equation ∂2g = 0, a so-called harmonic gauge transformation, does
not change it. Imposing as boundary conditions that the (gauge) ﬁelds vanishes at (spatial)
inﬁnity removes also this ambiguity, and ﬁxes the gauge completely.
In fact, it can be useful to leave a certain degree of gauge freedom. E. g., the Coulomb
gauge condition ∂iAi = 0 is only deﬁned up to purely time-dependent gauge transforma-
tions. There is thus a whole class of functions satisfying the Coulomb gauge condition.
Another examples are axial gauges with some ﬁxed vector nµ such that nµAµ = 0. Such
gauges can already simplify calculations considerably in certain cases.
In classical physics, it is not necessary to discuss further the residual freedom. Any will
do equally well, and often one can do without entirely. This will need to be discussed in
more detail in the quantum theory, which will be done in section 2.6.2, where the situation
is somewhat more involved.
It should, however, be noted that any term which would give a mass to the gauge
bosons, e. g. mAµAµ, cannot be added to the Lagrangian, as it will immediately break
gauge invariances, as can be seen immediately by calculation. In fact, (2.4) is the unique

Some basics of group theory
gauge-invariant Lagrangian perturbatively renormalizable by power-counting in four di-
mensions. Thus, any additional interactions compatible with gauge invariance would lead
to a much more involved theory. Thus, Abelian gauge theories are non-interacting and
exactly massless without matter
2.3
Some basics of group theory
Maxwell theory was based on the idea that at every space-time points the elements of a
real vector could be changed by a gauge transformation given by a real, scalar function.
To cover also the other forces observed so far in nature requires to generalize this concept.
It turns out that it requires to allow for ﬁelds which are tensor-valued in some abstract
internal space, just like with global symmetries, rather than just real functions.
This
requires some basic amount of group theory and the associated theory of algebras, which
will be collected here. In fact, also the global case of section 2.1 can be put usually in the
context of group theory.
To repeat, start with a group. A group G is a set of elements g with an associative
operation ·, which maps two elements g and h of the set into an element of the set, g·h ∈G,
and there exists a unique identity element e as well as a unique inverse element g−1 to
every element g, such that g · g−1 = g−1 · g = e and e · g = g · e = g. Note that the
operation does not need, but can, be commutative. If it is not, g · h ̸= h · g, the group is
called non-Abelian. Otherwise the group is Abelian.
A mapping of the elements g of the set to a linear operator or tensor D(g) in a vector
space such that the group structure is conserved, D(g)D(h) = D(g · h), is called a repre-
sentation. Such a representation needs not to be faithful, i. e. D(g) = D(h) is allowed,
provided the action of the operation is still represented correctly. However, note that not
every group can be represented non-trivially, i. e. other than setting D(g) = 1 for all
g, in every vector space. Sometimes the vector space needs to have a certain number of
dimensions and/or needs to be complex.
Generalizations of Maxwell theory are then constructed by having ﬁelds which no longer
map a space-time position to a real number, but to elements related to the group structure
It is important to note that the corresponding vector space is not position space, but an
additional vector space.
For quantum ﬁeld theory the most relevant subclass of groups are the Lie groups. They
are characterized in the following way: Given a matrix representation of a Lie group, it is
possible to write
D(g) = eiτ aφa,
(2.11)

Chapter 2.
Gauge theories
9
with the τ a called the generators of the group and the φa are real numbers. The generators
can be chosen hermitian, i. e., τa = τ †
a. Note that, by deﬁnition1, D(g−1) = D(g)†. The
τ a form the basis of a vector space, with the real numbers as ﬁeld. Thus, the exponent
in (2.11) can be any element of this vector space. The algebras of Lie groups are called
Lie algebras.
Note that the representation (2.11) deﬁnes also a representation of the
generators, and thus the algebra in the same vector space. In the following, groups will be
denoted by capital letters, and algebras by small letters. Within this lecture only compact
Lie groups will be considered for non-space-time symmetries, for which in addition tr(τ aτ a)
is a real and symmetric matrix with positive spectrum.
For ﬁnite-dimensional Lie algebras there exists a deﬁnite number N of generators τ a,
a = 1, ..., N, giving the dimension of the algebra vector space. It is also deﬁned to be
the dimension of the group, N = dim G. It is important to note that the number N is
independent of the actual dimension of the vector space of the representation of the group
always the same. Thus, depending on the vector space in which the representation is
constructed, also the τ a will be matrices of diﬀerent dimensions. Note that because of
(2.11), a generic group element can be expanded for inﬁnitesimal φa as 1 + iτ aφa. Thus
the algebra describes inﬁnitesimal group elements around the identity. This implies that
the group is continuous . Continuous groups are a special case of groups, and compact Lie
groups are again a special group of continuous groups, as further restrictions apply.
It should be noted that the relation (2.11) is actually multivalued. For the same algebra
it is possible to groups diﬀering by a center, i. e. a set of elements commuting with all other
elements. These groups diﬀer in terms of their connectedness. There is for any algebra
always a unique covering group, i. e. a simply connected group of which all other groups
belonging to the same algebra are subgroups.
Moreover, for the representation to respect the group structure it can be shown that
the τ a are required to respect the commutation relations

τ a, τ b
= ifabcτ c.
with the totally anti-symmetric structure constants fabc. This algebra is fulﬁlled no matter
the dimensionality of the actual matrices. Thus, the algebra can eventually be deﬁned also
in an abstract way without choosing actual matrices. Because of this, an actual matrix
form of the generators is also called a representation. The structure constants fulﬁll the
Jacobi identity
fabefcde + f acef dbe + f adef bce = 0.
(2.12)
This identity will be used multiple times in this lecture.

2.3.
Some basics of group theory
It should be noted that multiple groups can have the same algebra. Groups having
the same algebra diﬀer by their center, ZG, which is the set of group elements z ∈ZG
which satisfy [z, D(g)] = 0 for all group elements g. E. g., it can be shown that SU(2) and
SO(3) both have the same algebra, and diﬀer only by the discrete group Z2 = {1, −1}.
The diﬀerence in the groups imply in which vector spaces a (faithful) representation is
possible in which form, and whether the representations form a simply connected space or
not. While interesting, these subtle points will only be returned to if need be, which is in
most cases in (quantum) ﬁeld theory not the case.
An example is the su(2) algebra, with N = 3, having thus three generators. As a
two-dimensional, complex representation the Pauli matrices,

Here, it should be noted once more that several vector spaces play a role: The space-time
vector space, the vector space in which the representation of a group exists, and the vector
space which is deﬁned by the algebra. In this example, the space-time vector space is
the usual d-dimensional one, the representation vector space is two-dimensional, and the
vector space deﬁned by the generators is three-dimensional. For this particular case

to create the representation of the group elements.
The most relevant algebras for the standard model are su(2) and su(3). Though poten-
tially any (Lie) algebra/group can be used to construct a valid quantum ﬁeld theory, this
lecture will mostly concentrate on the case of su(N). Thus, for the sake of simplicity, in
the following mostly the expressions for su(N) will be given. Others can be obtained in a
straightforward way. Maxwell theory can be considered as being derived from the algebra
formed by the real unity, yielding as algebra space the real numbers, called u(1), which by
exponentiation yields the unit circle. The u(1) group is the special case of all f abc being
zero, and the algebra being one-dimensional. This algebra is equivalent to so(2), and it is
the only non-compact group to be encountered in this lecture for an internal space.

In general, there are N 2 −1 base vectors for su(N), but the dependency for the other
algebras is diﬀerent.
Generators, which are diagonal as matrices, and therefore commute with each other,
are said to be in the Cartan subalgebra or subgroup of the algebra or group, respectively.
For su(2), this is only one generator, for su(3) there are two. The size of the Cartan
subalgebra is called the rank of the group.
The lowest-dimensional realizations of the commutation relations are the fundamental
representations of the algebra or group, of which there are rank many, but not necessarily
of the same dimensionality. Since the commutation relations are invariant under unitary
transformations, it is possible to select a particular convenient realization. Note, however,
that there may be more than one unitarily inequivalent fundamental representations in
the same vector space. For su(2), there is only one. For su(3), they are two, the second
one created by using the −λa∗matrices.
It also possible to give representations of the algebras with higher-dimensional matrices
than the fundamental ones. The next simple one is the so-called adjoint representations
with the matrices
(Aa)ij = −if a

number of further representations with increasing dimensionality, which will not be needed
here.

2.4
Orbit structure
This brief section will analyze some implications of groups and group structures when
applying group elements to something, usually the vectors of the vector space in which a
representation is deﬁned. It should be noted, however, that the following could be also
deﬁned without an actual representation, as long as the action of a group element on some-
thing is deﬁned. But within this lecture this will always be realized in a representation.
This formalism will later be used to represent gauge theories as collections of orbits.
2.4.1
Orbits
The basic concept is that of an orbit. Take a single vector v of the vector space in which
exists a representation. Then an orbit, sometimes also called group orbit, O = {D(g)v}
is deﬁned as the set of all vectors obtained under application of all group elements from
this single vector. In a sense, this generalizes the concept of a ray. An example are the
hyperspheres which are obtained under the application of the rotation groups on a single
vector pointing to the surface of the hypersphere, e. g. in R2 the orbit of (1, 0)T would be
(cos α, sin α)T under the two-dimensional rotation group.
This structure has a number of consequences.
First, any vector space on which a
representation acts can be decomposed into distinct orbits, though the number of orbits
does not need to be countable. Second, group invariants, i. e. elements of the vector space
satisfying D(g)v = v, are trivial, i. e. one element, orbits.
From the point of physics, it is useful that orbits can also be classiﬁed by their invariant
tensors. An invariant tensor of rank n is a tensor T in the representation space of the group
which satisﬁes D1(g)...Dn(g)T = T, i. e. it remains invariant under application of group
elements. E. g. in the simplest case of SO(n) and the vector representation, the invariant
tensor is proportional to the unit matrix. An invariant tensor yields a characterization of
an orbit, i. e. a quantity which is diﬀerent for diﬀerent orbits. In the example this is the
length of the vector, which is invariant under rotation, and thus characterizes an orbit.

14
2.5.
Yang-Mills theory
Since representations can have more than one invariant tensor, orbits may have more
than one invariant to characterize them. Then, two orbits do only agree if they agree in
all the quantities characterized by invariant tensors. E. g. for the tensor representation
of SU(3), build from matrices Vab = vavb with v in the fundamental representation, there
are two invariant tensors. Therefore, there are two quantities for any orbit which are
invariant under the action of the group. One is again the length. The other is a more
complicated quantity, which follows from the fact that every matrix of this representation
satisﬁes dc
abVab = 0, and d is hence a traceless, symmetric tensor.
2.4.2
(Non-)linear representations
Though most of the previous has dealt with linear representations, i. e. representations
where group elements g have been mapped to matrix representations D(g) such that they
act as
D(g)abxb
on the elements x of the representation (Hilbert) space. This is not necessary.
The only requirement for a representation is actually that it maintains the group com-
position law. Thus, it is possible to formulate a non-linear representation
fa(x, g),
provided
f(f(x, g), h) = f(x, g · h)
where the group composition is maintained. Then, this map is a non-linear representation.
Though non-linear representation of groups (and algebras) are rare, they are sometimes
encountered. This will be, e. g., the case in section 4.5.
2.5
Yang-Mills theory
It is now possible to generalize Maxwell theory to the basic building block of essentially all
gauge theories, Yang-Mills theory. This is done by upgrading the gauge ﬁelds of Maxwell
theory to a tensor product of a space-time vector ﬁeld times a Lie algebra. This will be
done here for an arbitrary gauge algebra, though the dimension of the algebra will be
referred to as colors. Specializing the gauge algebra will then deﬁne a concrete gauge
theory. E. g. for QCD it is su(3). Eﬀectively, the gauge ﬁeld will assign for each point in
space-time for every direction an element of a Lie algebra.

Chapter 2.
Gauge theories
15
How this happens is actually rather direct. In Maxwell theory, the gauge ﬁelds were
a product of the (trivial) generator of u(1), being 1, and the gauge ﬁeld Aµ. Thus, for
a theory including a Lie algebra the generator will be replaced by the generators of the
group, i. e., the gauge ﬁelds will be given as vector in the algebra at every space-time point
Aµ(x) = Aa
µ(x)τa.
(2.18)
Note that at this point the generators are not in any speciﬁc representation. Rather, the
gauge ﬁelds are currently vectors in the vector space of the algebra, and the Aa
µ are the
components. The x-dependence merely states that a diﬀerent vector of the algebra space
is associated with every space-time point. I. e. gauge ﬁelds are elements of the vector
space spanned by the generators of the Lie algebra.
Hence, there are dim G gauge ﬁelds in a Yang-Mills theory. Most of the following can
be done essentially completely abstract, i. e. without choosing a particular representation
for the generators. However, it is usually practical to chose a representation, usually the
lowest-dimensional one. Then the τ a are matrices of size the lowest-dimensional funda-
mental representation. E. g., for su(N) these are N × N matrices, like the Pauli matrices
(2.13) or the Gell-Mann matrices (2.14).
It then remains to construct a gauge-invariant action for Yang-Mills theory, which
generalizes Maxwell theory. This is again an axiomatic process, which can be motivated
by various geometric arguments, which will be discussed in section 2.6, but in the end
remain a postulate. It is experiment which vindicates the following.
Since the gauge ﬁeld is now Lie-algebra-valued, so will be any gauge transformation
function be a vector in algebra space, τ aωa(x), from which a group-valued unitary gauge
transformation
G(x) = exp (igτ aωa(x))
(2.19)
is obtained.
Again, this does not yet require to specify a representation.
The gauge
transformation is now deﬁned to act on the gauge ﬁelds as
Aµ →GAµG−1 + G∂µG−1.

Yang-Mills theory
fact, by involving both G and G−1, the gauge transformation (2.20) is blind to the center

Note that (2.20) is inhomogeneous, as is the Abelian version (2.7), in the sense that
a ﬁeld-independent term arises. The Dab

adjoint representation of the gauge group. The adjoint stems from the appearance of the
structure constants. Note, however, that this is a relation in terms of coordinates in the
algebra space, and is not yet requiring a choice of representation.
There are two remarkable facts about this. On the on hand, there appears an arbitrary
constant g in both (2.19) and (2.22). This constant will later turn out to take the place of
the conventional electric charge as the coupling constant of Yang-Mills theory. The second
is that the transformation is no longer linear, but there appears a product, even in the
inﬁnitesimal case, of the gauge ﬁeld and the gauge transformation function ωa. It arises
from the non-Abelian structure of the gauge algebra, yielding non-zero structure constants
f abc. This non-linearity gives rise to all kind of technical complications.
This more involved structure requires also a change of the ﬁeld-strength tensor, to
obtain a gauge-invariant theory in the end. The ﬁeld strength tensor of Yang-Mills theory
is
Fµν = ∂µAν −∂νAµ −ig[Aµ, Aν] = F a

(2.23)
The ﬁeld-strength tensor is again a vector in the gauge algebra, but a symmetric tensor
in coordinate space. There are two more remarkable facts about this ﬁeld strength tensor.
One is that it is no longer linear in the gauge ﬁelds, but that there appears an interaction
term: Gauge ﬁelds in a Yang-Mills theory interact with each other, and the theory is even
without matter non-trivial. Furthermore, the appearance of g conﬁrms its interpretation
as a coupling constant. The second is that a straightforward calculation shows that this
expression is not gauge-invariant, in contrast to Maxwell theory. The reason is the non-
commutativity of the algebra-valued gauge ﬁelds.

18
2.6.
Where the gauge freedom comes from
Another consequence of the gauge-variance of the ﬁeld strength tensor is that the
color-electric ﬁeld and the color-magnetic ﬁeld are gauge-variant as well, and they can
thus not be measured: Yang-Mills theories do not manifest themselves as observable ﬁelds
nor as observable color waves. From this follows also that color charge is gauge-variant
and thus not observable, in contrast to electric charge. The only type of gauge-invariant
observables in Yang-Mills theory are necessarily composites of the gauge ﬁelds, like the
action.
However, after ﬁxing a gauge it is of course possible to make statements also
about the gauge bosons, and even use experiments to indirectly say something about the
properties of gauge bosons in a particular gauge. Much more will be said on this topic
during this lecture.
There is an interesting consequence of (2.23). It is possible to rescale A →gA, thereby
rescaling ¯F = gF. As a consequence, the rescaling allows to rewrite (2.25) as
L = −1
4g2tr( ¯Fµν ¯F µν),
(2.26)
where thus the coupling constant only acts as a mere prefactor. This remains true in the
presence of matter, as will be seen in section 2.7. Thus, the amplitude of the gauge ﬁeld
and the coupling become exchangeable concepts. Moreover, any expansion in the coupling
can be equally well be considered as an amplitude expansion.
2.6
Where the gauge freedom comes from
2.6.1
The Abelian case
As noted in section 2.5, it is possible to attach a geometrical interpretation to gauge ﬁelds.
For this, it is best to understand a little bit more what gauge transformations and gauge
ﬁxing really are.
The deﬁnition (2.18) is actually the statement that at every point in space a tensor
in a vector space is attached. I. e., for every point in space exists a tensor, in this case a
rank one tensor and thus a vector, in an additional vector space. This is the vector space
formed by the Lie algebra. Note that the gauge ﬁelds are the components of the vector,
while the generators are the (base) vectors. At that point, no representation needs to be
chosen for the generators themselves, and they can remain abstract.
Consider ﬁrst the Abelian case. Then, the generator is the unit operator, and the Lie
algebra space attached is a real vector space. At ﬁrst sight, it appears that there is one
such vector for each direction. This is, however, not true. A Lorentz vector is in the
(1/2, 1/2) representation of the Lorentz group. According to the Wigner-Eckart theorem

Chapter 2.
Gauge theories
19
there can be thus only two non-group-theoretical degrees of freedom, multiplying the two
invariant tensors, one for each of the two SU(2) subgroups. That is to be expected from
classical physics, where it had already been seen that electromagnetic waves have only two
physical degrees of freedom. So, it needs to be possible to decompose the vector potential
accordingly.
The Abelian gauge ﬁeld transforms according to (2.7), Aµ →A′
µ = Aµ + ∂µg. If the
gauge ﬁeld is contracted with a transverse projector, this yields for the gauge-transformed
ﬁeld in momentum space

and thus carries all the changes, but does also include (longitudinal) information from the
gauge ﬁeld. This reduces the degrees of freedom, leaving one. Since the gauge ﬁeld is
furthermore in the massless representation of the translation group, any momentum of a
physical degree of freedom is necessarily lightlike. Thus, for momentum P = (p, 0, 0, p),
the gauge ﬁeld can be decomposed as A = (A0, A1, A2, A0) = A⊥+ A0. Because
Fµν = ϵµνρσpρAσ

the so deﬁned ﬁeld is indeed gauge-invariant. However, it is not a local object anymore,
as the second integral in (2.27) cannot be eliminated. Thus passing to a physical vector
ﬁeld implies even in QED giving up locality.
Note that the physical ﬁeld is entirely
transverse, as any other contraction of it vanishes by construction.
In particular, the
light-like component A0 drops out as well. Thus, it carries only two degrees of freedom
(in four dimensions). In fact, as the non-local term is essentially a solution to the partial
diﬀerential equations determining the vector potential at ﬁxed electric and magnetic ﬁeld,
it is possible to regain an expression in terms of these ﬁelds.
In lower dimensions, the Lorentz group reduces ﬁrst to SO(1, 2), and thus there is
only a single non-trivial degree of freedom, due to the Wigner-Eckart theorem. Again,
the longitudinal component and timelike component can be eliminated in the same way.
Finally, in two dimensions, the Lorentz group is itself Abelian, SO(1, 1). The situation
is quite odd, as spin can non be continous, as the representations are labelled contin-
uously. However, longitudinal components and time-like components do not contribute
for the same reasons, and thus the gauge ﬁeld has no physical degrees of freedom in two
dimensions. Likewise, in higher dimensions, the degrees of freedom increase to be always
dimensionality−2.
This leaves the question open, why only one gauge condition is necessary, rather than
two. In fact, the previous example showed this already. Imposing, e. g., Landau gauge
∂µAµ = 0 is making the gauge ﬁeld already transverse without projection.
But then
Lorentz symmetry requires that the light-like part drops out. In a sense, the gauge condi-
tion ﬁxes the relative distribution of freedom between both redundant degrees of freedom.
The elimination of them is ultimately performed by the underlying Lorentz symmetry.
This is not necessary, as is demonstrated by the fact that the vector potential is never
necessary to introduce. Thus, it is possible to work without ﬁxing this relation. But if

Chapter 2.
Gauge theories
21
the vector potential should be sued in calculation, this distribution is necessary to have a
unique deﬁnition of the ﬁeld. As this ﬁxes the relative orientation of the vectors in algebra
space, this can be thought of a ﬁxing the coordinates in algebra space.
In principle, this can be done ultralocally. E. g., axial gauges work by introducing a
vector n and demanding nA = c, where c is a constant, often set to zero. However, these
conditions are not frame-independent, as introducing a ﬁxed vector n makes Poincar´e
symmetry hidden. As a consequence, degrees of freedom need to be decomposed with
respect to n, making calculations often quite demanding, and especially ﬁnding consistent
approximations become challenging. Gauges like Landau gauge, on the other hand, allow
to have manifest Lorentz Poincar´e symmetries at all stages, at the expense of no longer
being ultralocal, but merely local.
Thus, the number of degrees of freedom is determined due to the Lorentz represen-
tation.
Each of the invariant tensors carries one vector in the algebra.
For the same
reason the situation is the same in the Yang-Mills case. However, because of its non-linear
transformation (2.21), this becomes less obvious.
Especially, (2.27) does not create a
gauge-invariant ﬁeld, and also is the ﬁeld-strength tensor gauge-invariant. In fact, because
of the non-linear part, it will explicitly depend on the other components as well.
But why bother at all? Why not work exclusively with invariant quantities, like (2.27)?
After all, this is what classical mechanics teaches: Any remaining degree of freedom is
nothing but a redundancy which can be eliminated by passing to suitable generalized
coordinates. And classical electrodynamics shows that is indeed possible. The answer to
this is one of practicality. Some things have even been calculated in this way, especially
the (in)famous Aharanov-Bohm eﬀect. But in general, this becomes practically essentially
impossible.
The reasons are the arising non-localities, such as those in (2.27).
Thus,
introducing redundancy by having gauge ﬁelds as auxiliary degrees of freedom, paying
with the appearance of the internal vector space, is practically the better choice. While
non-locality is not a real conceptual problem, this is an almost insurmountable practical
one. This applies to general statements even more than to actual calculations. The obvious
follow-up question is then why this kind of redundant variables, and not some other. The
answer is that this has been the one that works. Others may exist, but none has been
found making it easier.
2.6.2
The non-Abelian case and the Gribov-Singer ambiguity
When passing to Yang-Mills theory, the situation becomes only technically more involved.
The simple translations of (2.7) become replaced by the non-linear transformation (2.20)
in the vector space spanned by the Lie algebra space. While on a conceptual level, this

22
2.6.
Where the gauge freedom comes from
does not change anything, there arises a serious technical problem. This makes it also
harder to see that indeed there are only two degrees of freedom per generator even in this
case. The problem arises from the fact that a Lie algebra describes a manifold, and for
non-Abelian (compact) Lie algebras this manifold does not allow for a trivial atlas. Thus,
transfer functions are needed. However, because implementing a gauge choice is becoming
space-time dependent, the transfer functions will also become space-time dependent. In
this way, the global structure of the Lie algebra space aﬀect also space-time quantities.
At the root of the problem is the so-called Gribov problem, or more generally the
Gribov-Singer ambiguity. It is a genuine non-perturbative feature, and therefore does not
arise in perturbative treatments of Yang-Mills theory.
The main problem starts with the realization that local gauge conditions do not have
unique solutions beyond perturbation theory in the non-Abelian case.
In general, the
question of uniqueness needs to be formulated in the following way. Consider ﬁrst the
Abelian case. Given a gauge condition C[Aµ, x] = 0 and arbitrary gauge ﬁeld ¯Aµ(x), does
there exist a unique solution g(x) to
C[ ¯Aµ + ∂µg, x] = 0?
Of course, the answer to this question will depend on the gauge condition. Provided that
C takes the form of a diﬀerential equation for Aµ, this is turned into a diﬀerential equation
for g(x). The question can thus be answered by the theory of partial diﬀerential equations.
E. g., in the case of the Landau gauge, this is an elliptic partial diﬀerential equation for
which uniqueness theorems exist, proving that there is one and only one solution for ﬁxed
boundary conditions.
In the non-Abelian case, this takes the form
C[ ¯Aa
µ + ∂µg, x]a = 0?
with g group-valued. Thus, these are coupled equations. Because the manifold has a non-
trivial atlas2, the existence theorems can no longer be applied. If only the surrounding of
an element is probed, which is the equivalent of a small amplitude expansion, this is no
problem. This is the situation for perturbative expansions in the coupling constant, due
to the equivalence of amplitude and coupling expansion. But if the whole manifold needs
to be probed, this no longer holds true. Then transfer functions are needed, and thus the
2The simplest example is the gauge group SU(2). It is equivalent to the surface of a 3-sphere. Such
a surface cannot be described with a single coordinate system, as it would be ill-deﬁned at least at one
pole, and at least two coordinate systems are necessary. This is also the reason why an Abelian gauge
theory is not aﬀected. U(1) is isomorphic to a circle, which can be covered by a single coordinate system.

Chapter 2.
Gauge theories
23
global manifold structure plays a role, obstructing a purely local gauge condition to yield
a unique answer.
Thus, by only implementing such a gauge, the vector potential is no longer single-
valued. This can be most easily seen in gauges where perturbatively there is one and
only solution, like in the Landau gauge. The additional solutions are called Gribov copies.
Thus, the statement of the existence of Gribov copies takes there the form that the gauge
condition ∂µAµ
a = 0 has more than one solution beyond perturbation theory. It is for this
important to realize that any non-perturbative treatment has to start from the gauge trans-
formation (2.20) rather than from the inﬁnitesimal one (2.21). Using the latter implies the
assumption that it is always possible to construct gauge transformations from consecutive
inﬁnitesimal ones. It is this assumption which actually breaks down in non-Abelian gauge
theories.
To be concrete, consider su(2) Yang-Mills theory. An example is given by the so-called
instanton ﬁeld conﬁguration

This can also be seen from the fact that the gauge transformation (2.29) is not continu-

24
2.7.
Matter, representations, and coupling universality
gauge transformation, it is a so-called large gauge transformation. This is actually neces-
sary. Since the perturbative construction proves that there is no inﬁnitesimally adjacent
gauge transformation to the Landau gauge, and it is therefore unique, any additional
gauge copies have to be separated by a large gauge transformation. There are many more
explicit examples known in the literature.
Of course, any space-time point is still associated to unique algebra vector along the
gauge orbit. It is thus still possible to specify a unique gauge copy. But the gauge condition
then usually takes the form of an integral equation. E. g., requiring to the gauge ﬁeld to
absolutely minimize
Z
d4xAa
µAµa
(2.29)
can be shown to yield a unique gauge copy on every gauge orbit.
Though this seems to be a formidable problem, it is rather a practical than a conceptual
problem. Using gauge conditions which involve integrals over the gauge ﬁelds, it is always
possible to construct gauge conditions, which are not ambiguous. It is straightforward to
implement some of these gauge conditions in numerical calculations. However, they be-
come very quickly very expensive in terms of computing time, it may even be exponentially
expensive, when the volume is increased. The reason is that using an integrated gauge
condition implies that an integral equation has to be satisﬁed, which becomes numerically
more expensive the more lattice points there are.
The analytical situation is even worse, and little is known about practical solutions to
the problem. However, the situation is still of technical nature in the classical theory. The
situation will be revisited in the quantized version in chapter (6), where the implications
are more far reaching.
2.7
Matter, representations, and coupling universal-
ity
2.7.1
Matter ﬁelds in Abelian gauge theories
So far, the theory only contained the gauge ﬁelds. It remains to introduce matter particles.
Again, this requires a postulate, which is then justiﬁed empirically. It is again useful to
start with the Abelian case ﬁrst.
Empirically, matter ﬁelds are required to transform under a gauge transformation as
φg(x) = eieg(x)φ(x),
(2.30)

Chapter 2.
Gauge theories
25
i. e. by a pure phase factor. In this a new, arbitrary, constant, e appears. It will be seen
that it plays the same role as the self-coupling in the Abelian case, but in the Abelian
case only pertains to the interactions of matter with the gauge ﬁeld. This also requires
the matter ﬁelds to be complex. A term not involving derivatives, e. g. a mass term φ†φ,
is trivially invariant under a gauge transformation.
This is diﬀerent for expressions involving derivatives, like kinetic terms,
∂µφg = eieg (∂µ + ie∂µg) φ.
Typical kinetic terms are found to be (∂µφ)†∂µφ and ¯ψiγµ
ij∂µψj, where the Γµ are ﬁxed
matrices, the so-called Dirac matrices. These are terms for bosonic particles and fermionic
particles, respectively, which will be discussed in more detail in the lecture quantum ﬁeld
theory I. At the moment, details beyond the derivative nature are not relevant.
What is relevant is that neither term can be alone gauge-invariant. This can be changed
by introducing a covariant derivative
Dg
µφg = (∂µ −ieAg
µ)φg = eieg (∂µ + ie∂g −ieAµ + ie∂µg) φ = eiegDµφ,
(2.31)
which makes derivatives transform like like a matter ﬁeld. Thus, by replacing all derivatives
by covariant derivatives a gauge-invariant Lagrangian of Maxwell ﬁelds and matter is
obtained. As this is the simplest, but not the only, possibility to do so, this is called
minimal coupling. Indeed, it is again experiment which tells that this is correct.
It should be noted that (2.31) induces an interaction vertex between the matter and
the gauge ﬁeld, with a coupling strength e.
At lowest order in the low-energy limit,
the so-called Thomson limit, this is just the classical interaction of an electron with the
electromagnetic ﬁeld. This justiﬁes to call this quantity electric charge. It should be noted
that for every matter ﬁeld there can be arbitrarily diﬀerent electric charges, as long as the
respective covariant derivatives and gauge transformations use the same. Thus, electric
charge is not ﬁxed by the theory, but is an experimental input.
2.7.2
Matter ﬁelds and coupling universality in non-Abelian gauge
theories
After having the coupling of matter ﬁelds to Abelian gauge ﬁelds the next step is to
generalize it to coupling to Yang-Mills ﬁelds.
Again, it is for the moment irrelevant
whether the matter is (pseudo)scalars, fermions, or have other spin assignments, though
this of course inﬂuences details of the Lagrangian structure to form Lorentz scalars.
In addition, the following will use the concept of minimal coupling, which is a direct
generalization of minimal coupling in the Abelian case, (2.31). I. e. it will work using a

26
2.7.
Matter, representations, and coupling universality
covariant derivative only slightly generalized from the Abelian version. While there may
be other possibilities to introduce gauge theories so far this is the only type consistent
with any experimentally known theory. For this reason, this lecture is exclusively devoted
to this class of gauge theories. They are also arguably the simplest version, and still more
than complicated enough to be not fully understood until today.
For an Abelian group structure, every irreducible representation is one-dimensional. I.
e. everything boils down to sets of independent functions, not vectors. Thus, everything
ultimately boils down to allow for phase rotations of the matter ﬁelds under a gauge
transformation, φ →eigω(x)φ, where g is some arbitrary constant. This implies that φ
needs to be complex. In fact, from the vantage point of global symmetries of section 2.1,
this is having a global U(1) symmetry, and making it local. This is indeed also the usual
way of creating a gauge theory: Take a matter theory with global symmetry, and making
the group, or some subgroup of it, local3. Now, any generalization of phase transitions
will still leave combinations of the type φ†φ invariant under a gauge transformation, but
not a kinetic term involving ∂µ. Thus, ordinary kinetic terms involving ∂µφ are no longer
gauge-invariant. But this need to be.
The origin of this problem is that ∂µ is not only involving the ﬁeld φ at some position x.
Because of the limiting procedure deﬁning a derivative, it also involves information from
some nearby region. But because of the gauge freedom, the coordinate systems in algebra
space or group can even at two nearby points be diﬀerent. And since the ﬁeld components
are the vector components in algebra space they can only be combined if calculated in the
same coordinate system.
It is thus necessary to somehow compensate for the possible change of coordinate
system. In the Abelian case this can be achieved by introducing the covariant derivative
Dµ, which has the property that under a gauge transformation it behaves as Dµφ →
eigω(x)Dµφ, i. e. that applied to the ﬁeld the whole object transforms as a ﬁeld. Then, the
combinations appearing in kinetic terms become again gauge-invariant. Because of this
the covariant derivative is also called parallel transporter, because it makes the changes
of the ﬁeld comparable to changes of the ﬁeld locally.
This requires that the covariant derivative itself transforms as
Dµ →eigω(x)Dµe−igω(x).
If it would be just the ordinary derivative, this would yield
eigω(x)∂µe−igω(x) = ∂µ −ig∂µω(x).
3If a subgroup is made local, and the original group was not simple, some global group remains.
However, this will not be relevant throughout most of this lecture, but plays a very important role in
actual theories.

Chapter 2.
Gauge theories
27
Thus, a mismatch ∂µω(x) arises. But this is precisely of the same form as (2.7). Thus, a
covariant derivative deﬁned as
Dµ = ∂µ + igAµ
(2.32)
has exactly the desired properties. Because this implies that the gauge ﬁeld connects the
nearby points to compensate for the change of coordinate system, the gauge ﬁeld is also
called connection in a more mathematical context. And this is exactly what appears also
in (2.31) in the Abelian case. It should be noted that the constant g has been arbitrary,
but parametrizes the strength of the coupling between the gauge ﬁeld and the matter
ﬁeld. However, as long as the constant is the same for the gauge transformation and the
covariant derivative, it is arbitrary, and needs to be measured to be ﬁxed. At the same
time, it can be diﬀerent for diﬀerent matter ﬁelds.
This is the procedure of minimal coupling: Chose a matter ﬁeld with global symmetry
group, gauge the symmetry group or a subgroup, and ﬁll in covariant derivatives with
couplings taken from experiment. That’s it, this is how the standard program for con-
structing a gauge theory from a non-gauge theory works. It should be noted, however,
that even if the gauge ﬁelds are redundant degrees of freedom, the ungauged and gauged
theories diﬀer qualitatively. If passing to appropriate generalized coordinates it is not the
ungauged theory, which arises, but a more complicated one. Moreover, the matter ﬁelds
are now gauge-dependent. Thus, just as the gauge ﬁelds themselves, they depend on a
choice of coordinate system, and are hence no longer physical degrees of freedom, as they
were in the ungauged theory. How to remove the gauge ﬁeld again will be discussed later,
and what then are physical degrees of freedom.
The recipe to put matter into non-Abelian Yang-Mills theories is quite similar, but for
two important details. The ﬁrst is that a non-Abelian gauge theory has many diﬀerent
(irreducible) representations, and especially many higher-dimensional ones.
It is thus
necessary to chose the representation for the theory with the global symmetry. Any choice
is possible, and experiment will dictate which one is necessary.
Choosing some linear representation R, this representation is n-dimensional. The gen-
erators in this representation are then N n × n matrices τ R
a , and group elements can be
written as exp(igτ R
a ωa(x)/2). The factor 2 is here a convenient normalization. Any matter
ﬁeld is then an n-dimensional vector4 φi. A gauge transformation then acts as

This has very far reaching consequences for any particle physics theory. It determines
that the interaction strength of the gauge bosons and between the matter and the gauge
bosons is uniquely related. This is a unambiguous prediction, which is experimentally
testable. The other is that not every classical non-Abelian gauge theory can be consistently

29
quantized, as will be discussed in chapter 7. The latter has, e. g., implications to the
equality of the electric charges of electrons and protons, as is discussed in the lectures on
electroweak physics or physics beyond the standard model.
In much of the following, the gauge algebra/group will be su(N)/SU(N) with matter in
the fundamental representation. This is the situation encountered in the standard model.
It is also technically often the simplest case. A generalization to arbitrary representations
and gauge algebras/group is usually straightforward, often just a replacement of numerical
factors and constant matrices.
There is one important distinction to note.
If a representation is not complex, as
is the case for all representations of SU(N > 2), but (pseudo)real, matter particles and
antiparticles are not independent. Rather, they are the same, possibly up to a phase. This
will play an important role in section 6.3.2, where physical states, especially bound states,
will be constructed.
It has also a second implication, if there are multiple ﬁelds in the same representation.
If there are multiple ﬁelds in the same representation and with otherwise the same quantum
numbers they can be transformed into each other by an additional symmetry transforma-
tion6. This is called a ﬂavor symmetry7. The group associated with this symmetry will
depend on whether the gauge group representation of the matter ﬁelds is complex, real, or
pseudoreal. If the matter is fermionic, this ﬂavor symmetry can also be combined with the
corresponding chiral symmetry in diﬀerent ways, depending on the type of representation.
However, as this is otherwise not part of the gauge symmetry, and the possibilities quickly
proliferate, this issue is mostly treated in lectures for particular implementations of gauge
theories.

they are gauge-invariant. E. g. a term (φ†

The general case is dependent on the ﬁelds and representations, and quickly becomes an
exercise in group theory applications. This will not be pursued further here, but will be a
substantial part in the lectures applied to speciﬁc theories, e. g. the standard model.
2.8
Beyond Yang-Mills theory
While the bulk of this lecture focuses on Yang-Mills theory, it is useful to generalize the
concept of gauge theories. These are often technically more involved, and more will be
said in section 4.6. Here, primarily, other possible gauge theory structures will be exposed.
In the following, matter will not be considered.

Chapter 2.
Gauge theories
31
2.8.1
Yang-Mills type
To set the stage, consider again the type encountered so far, Yang-Mills type. The gauge
transformation takes the form
Aa
µ(x)′
=

(2.34)
δϵAa
µ(x)
=
Dµϵa(x) = ∂µϵa + f a

(2.35)
where ϵc(x) is a set of arbitrary functions, the gauge transformations. These are algebra-
valued vectors. In these theories, the commutator of inﬁnitesimal gauge transformations
reads
[δε, δη] X = δξX,
ξa = f a
bcεbηc
(2.36)
with ε, η, ξ are arbitrary gauge transformations, and where X can be any ﬁeld.
It
follows that the algebra of the gauge transformations closes, i. e. is valid, oﬀ-shell as the
commutator of the gauge transformations is again a gauge transformation of the same
type. Alternatively, this could be stated in the form that the algebra holds for any values
of the ﬁelds.
2.8.2
Closure only on-shell
Oﬀ-shell closure holds for Yang-Mills gauge theories but is not a general feature of gauge
systems. The gauge transformations might close only when the equations of motion hold.
Notable examples where this is the case are extended supergravity theories. Rather than
discussing the gauge structure of supergravities, which is rather intricate, “closure only
on-shell” is illustrated in the case of a much simpler (but of no direct physical interest)
system. Start with the Lagrangian
L = 1
2( ˙q1 −˙q2 −˙q3)2 = 1
2 ˙y2,
y = q1 −q2 −q3
(2.37)
for a model with three coordinates q1, q2 and q3. L is invariant under two diﬀerent sets of
gauge transformations, which can be taken to be
δεq1 = ε + εq2¨y, δεq2 = ε, δεq3 = εq2¨y
(2.38)
and
δηq1 = 0, δηq2 = η, δηq3 = −η
(2.39)
The commutators of the gauge transformations on the ﬁelds are given by
[δε, δη] q1 = εη¨y,
[δε, δη] q2 = 0,
[δε, δη] q3 = εη¨y.
(2.40)

32
2.8.
Beyond Yang-Mills theory
From (2.37), the equation of motion for y is ¨y = 0.
Thus, the algebra of the gauge
transformations (2.38) and (2.39) is closed (and, in fact, Abelian) only up to equations of
motion, i.e., only on-shell. Otherwise, it cannot be expressed only in terms of the ﬁelds
qi, but requires their derivatives, and thus canonical conjugated momenta, as well.
2.8.3
Reducible gauge theories
The gauge transformations might also be “reducible”, i.e., dependent. Consider a theory
of an Abelian antisymmetric tensor ﬁeld Bµν = −Bνµ.
Following the construction of
Yang-Mills theory, the ﬁeld strength tensor is now rank three and is found to be
Hµνρ = ∂µBνρ + ∂νBρµ + ∂ρBµν.
A Lagrangian
L = −1
12HµνρHµνρ
(2.41)
can be constructed which is invariant under gauge transformations
δΛBµν = ∂µΛν −∂νΛµ
where Λ is the gauge parameter. These transformations vanish for a class of parameters
Λµ = ∂µϵ
(2.42)
meaning that the gauge transformations Λµ are not all independent. Such gauge trans-
formations are called reducible, and the corresponding gauge theory is said to be re-
ducible. Such antisymmetric tensor ﬁelds deﬁne a natural generalization of electromag-
netism, Aµ →Bµν and occur in many models of uniﬁcation. The main diﬀerence with
electromagnetism being the irreducibility of the latter.
Reducibility could be stacked. If, e. g., ϵ in (2.42) in turn obeys some equation, this
would be a second level of reducibility, and so on.
2.8.4
Reducibility on-shell
The last version is the possibility that the reducibility of the gauge transformations holds
only on-shell. This therefore combines the previous two cases. Adding to (2.41) an auxil-
iary ﬁeld Aµ leads to
L = 1
12AµεµνρσHνρσ −1
8AµAµ.
This Lagrangian reduces to (2.41) by inserting the equation of motion for Aµ,
Aµ = 1
3εµνρσHνρσ.

Gauge theories
33
The Lagrangian is invariant under gauge transformations of the form
δΛBµν
=
∂µΛν −∂νΛµ
(2.43)
δΛAµ
=
0.
(2.44)
Thus, nothing yet happened for the Abelian case. Upgrading this to a non-Abelian case
by considering, like in the Yang-Mills case, Lie-algebra-valued ﬁelds Aµ = Aa
µTa and
Bµν = Ba
µντa requires again to introduce covariant derivatives instead of partial derivatives,
∂µ →Dµ. This is the so-called Freedman-Townsend model.
It can now be shown that in this model the gauge transformations vanish for parameters
Λµ = Dµϵ. But now, this vanishing occurs only if the equations of motion are satisﬁed.
This is due to [Dµ, Dν] ∝Fµν, and Fµν = 0 is the equation of motion for B. The theory
is said in that case to be reducible on-shell.
2.8.5
Towards gravity
Finally, it should be remarked that if one would allow for position-dependent translations
in M4, very much like the position dependent transformations in algebra space, this would
yield general relativity. Thus, the concept coming with gauge theories underlie the two
central theories of modern physics, the standard model of particle physics and general
relativity. All of the things said here then also apply to space-time and space-time co-
ordinates, and it is necessary to pass to coordinate-invariant quantities. This is already
the case in classical general relativity, as much as in classical electrodynamics. Also there
one needs to work with gauge-invariant quantities, the generalization of electromagnetic
ﬁelds, and no longer with coordinates, as they are no longer physical. How to do so at
the quantum level has not yet been fully understand, and this is the quest for quantum
gravity. This is the topic of a diﬀerent lecture.

Chapter 3
Classical solutions
While gauge theories are needed to be quantized, classical solutions of them can still be of
great relevance. This is usually true for two reasons. One is that quantum eﬀects can be
small, and be treated as perturbations around the classical solutions. Typical examples of
this approach is already the WKB approximation in quantum mechanics. Another feature
is that properties of classical solutions may still be useful even in the quantized theory. E.
g. the wavelength of classical electromagnetic waves can be transferred to characterize the
properties of radiation in the quantum theory.
3.1
Time evolution and initial conditions
Classical gauge theories are partial diﬀerential equations, usually (non-linear) wave equa-
tions. Due to their deﬁnition on Minkowski space-time, they are accessible to existence
and uniqueness proofs. However, there is a twist for gauge theories, due to the gauge
freedom.
Consider ﬁrst the Abelian case. The Maxwell equations (2.8-2.9). The equation (2.8)
constitutes four equations of motion, while (2.9) provides four more constraint equations.
In terms of the gauge-invariant electric ﬁeld and magnetic ﬁeld they take the form of the
usual Maxwell equations. In the end, only two, out of the six, degrees of freedom remain
independent.
Four are eliminated by constraint equations, and two of the dynamical
equations turn out to be identically fulﬁlled if the other two are fulﬁlled.
The remaining second-order diﬀerential equations are relativistic wave-equations. To
ﬁnd a unique solutions requires initial condition. These can be chosen to give the inde-
pendent ﬁeld components and their derivatives on a space-like hypersurface. The Maxwell
equation allows to evolve the ﬁeld values then along time-like trajectories. The initial
conditions fully speciﬁc the ﬁelds at all space-time points. While this may be technically
34

Chapter 3.
Classical solutions
35
cumbersome in practice, this is the full solution. E. g. a spatial plane wave on a space-like
hypersurface will give rise to free electromagnetic plane waves.
The situation changes drastically once the problem is expressed in terms of the vector
potential. The constraint equation (2.9) is then trivially satisﬁed. The dynamical equation
(2.8) takes the form
∂2Aν −∂ν∂µAµ = 0,
(3.1)
and thus take the form of a coupled set of equations. However, even when specifying the
vector potential on a hypersurface at t = 0, any vector potential of the form
Aµ(t,⃗x) + ∂µg(t,⃗x) with ∂µg(0,⃗x) = 0
(3.2)
is an equally good solution.
Thus, the solution becomes many-valued.
The reason is
that the dynamical equations with the trivially resolved constraint equation can no longer
uniquely determine the time evolution.
Of course, at the physical level this doe snot
matter, as (3.2) is just a gauge transformation. Put it diﬀerently, the time evolution of
the vector potential is only unique up to gauge transformation. This can (only) be resolved
by requiring that the gauge ﬁeld satisﬁes a suitable gauge condition at all times. Thus, a
gauge condition is an additional constraint equation for the time evolution of the vector
potential, which needs to be satisﬁed by the initial conditions as well. Thus, a human
choice, the gauge condition, is required to make the time evolution of the vector potential
unique.
This, probably better than anything else, shows that the vector potential is an auxil-
iary degree of freedom introduced to simplify calculations. However, without that, many
calculations would be impossible. If, e. g., the Landau gauge condition is speciﬁed, the
last term in (3.1) drops out, and the equation is a free wave equation. Its time evolution
is then again unique, and determined by the initial condition. In particular, it is deter-
ministic. The whole problem can also be seen in a diﬀerent way. If the time evolution is
done in terms of the electric and magnetic ﬁeld, it is, without gauge condition, possible
to introduce an inﬁnite number of diﬀerent gauge ﬁelds. Only once a gauge condition is
added, this becomes unique.
This provides, however, another important lesson: It is impossible to preserve gauge
freedom and having a unique time evolution of the vector potential simultaneously. A
unique, deterministic time evolution of the vector potential has a ﬁxed gauge, and thus
the gauge symmetry is no longer manifest.

36
3.2.
Topological excitations
3.2
Topological excitations
All of these statements about the Abelian case apply equally well to the non-Abelian case,
except that even the non-Abelian electromagnetic ﬁelds are no longer gauge-invariant,
and also do not evolve uniquely in time without ﬁxing a gauge. Only quantities like the
action are invariant, and show a unique evolution in time. It requires twice the number of
generators of such quantities, for which the equations of motions need to be reconstructed.
For them to be gauge-invariant requires (at least) products of ﬁelds, for which then time
evolution equations need to be deduced from the Maxwell equations.
There is one issue, however, which makes this more problematic. Due to the Gribov-
Singer ambiguity, gauge conditions are not local in space-time. An gauge condition like
(2.29) is not local in time. Fixing such a gauge turns the time evolution equation into an
integral equation, and makes the causal structure no longer appearing. Even when ﬁxing
the gauge only on a spatial hyper surface, this will generally require non-local conditions,
connecting casually disconnected quantities. Of course, the vector potential is unphysical,
and may have acausal correlations, as long as observables evolve entirely causal. This
makes such an approach very involved indeed.
As will be seen in chapter 4, in the quantum theory entanglement will anyhow require
to deal with such correlations, as in all quantum theories.
After these preliminaries, the question is what are classical solutions.
For this, it
will be useful to take a somewhat indiﬀerent stand: Finding solutions for given initial
conditions, but allow time evolution to change the gauge, without imposing it. As it turns
out, for suitable chosen initial conditions there exists a time evolution such that (2.29) is
maintained.
The results will indeed be relevant even in the quantum theory, especially in chapter
7. While matter particles, gauge bosons and bound states are states which consists out of
a few valence particles, i. e. particles which deﬁne their quantum numbers, non-Abelian
gauge theories can also support other states. Especially, it can support states which are
genuine collective excitations of gauge bosons, with or without matter, which can no longer
be identiﬁed individually. Such states are usually highly unstable. However, some such
excitations are stabilized by geometric eﬀects, and then called topological excitations, for
reasons to be explored in the following. Such topological solutions are indeed the building
blocks of classical solutions of Yang-Mills theory, very much like the plane waves are in
Abelian gauge theories.
The most prominent of these excitations are so-called instantons. Though it is not
possible to directly observe instantons, their eﬀects can be identiﬁed.
To understand
them, it is best to ﬁrst investigate similar excitations in quantum mechanics.

Classical solutions

3.2.
Topological excitations
The not-total-derivative terms are again symmetric in two indices and hence vanish. The
total derivatives are symmetric about three indices and hence do not vanish in all cases,
but only in two out of three, and hence the result is 4/3. The total result is then
1

The last term vanished, because the square of the potential cannot provide a contribution
over the whole space because otherwise the ﬁelds would not vanish. The remaining last
term is a result of the commutator of the generators and the factors of the generators, the
gauge ﬁelds.
Evidently, (3.10) is a total derivative, and hence can be cast into a surface integral at
inﬁnity. It is therefore independent of the internal structure of the ﬁelds in the space-time it
is integrated over, but depends only on the contribution from the boundary. Furthermore,
the expression has the same color structure as the usual Lagrangian, and the Lorentz
indices do not play a role in gauge transformations of the ﬁeld-strength tensor. Hence,
this quantity is gauge-invariant. Thus, it is an observable quantity. It is the so-called
topological charge, or Chern class, of the gauge ﬁeld conﬁguration.
Furthermore, the
quantity is evidently invariant under any continuous distortions of the gauge ﬁelds inside
the volume. It is less obvious that this is true for any continuous deformations of the
gauge ﬁelds on the boundary, and that all of these possible deformations fall into distinct
classes, the so-called Chern classes, such that the integral is an integer k, characterizing
this class. This fact is stated here without proof. However, this can be seen intuitively.
Because the gauge ﬁelds are given by (3.8) with U becoming the identity at inﬁnity, at most
the derivatives of the U can play a role. These depend only on trigonometric functions of
angles, when sweeping the surface at inﬁnity. To have a non-zero value, it is thus necessary
that suitable multiples of periods are sampled, thus yielding a discretized result.
Hence nw is an integer, and enumerates an inﬁnite set of classical vacua. Since they
are topological diﬀerent, there is no path from one vacuum to the other where the energy
remains zero all the way. At the quantum level tunneling can connect topological diﬀerent
vacuum states. But there are also classical ﬁnite energy solutions.
